--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 for every STATIONARY robot → want robots MOVING
- After switch time: score += 1 for every MOVING robot → want robots STOPPED
- Switch happens between steps 400-600, signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up
- The green wall (color 2 in ARGoS? Let me check: clr options are 1-6, and the condition C=7 looks for colors 1-6)

Wait, let me re-read: the wall lights up GREEN. In ARGoS, green is typically color 2. The condition C=7 checks for a perceived color with `--l<I>x<K>` in {1,2,3,4,5,6}.

**Optimal Strategy:**
1. Before switch: robots should MOVE (exploration/attraction/repulsion)
2. After switch: robots should STOP
3. Trigger: perceive GREEN color (color 2) → switch from moving to stopped

**Design:**
- State 0: Move (Exploration or Attraction) - before switch
- State 1: Stop - after switch
- Transition from State 0 to State 1: when GREEN is perceived (C=7, l=2)
- Transition from State 1 back to State 0: maybe with very low probability (in case of false negative)

The champion (all-stop, score ~5000) is doing well because it's mostly stopped. But the optimal should be: move before switch (~400-600 steps × 10 robots = 4000-6000 points saved), then stop after. Actually, moving before saves those points but costs points after. The champion stops all the time scoring ~5000 (roughly 500 steps × 10 robots for the "before" portion). If we could move before and stop after, we'd score 0 ideally.

**Key insight:** The green wall perception is crucial. State 0 = Exploration (moving), transitions to State 1 = Stop when green is seen.

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Champion (elitism):** 1-state Stop
2-7. **Exploitation of the key idea:** 2-state FSM: Explore → Stop on green
8-14. **Exploration:** Various topologies

For green color: ARGoS typically uses color indices where green=2. Let me use l=2 for green.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**What's working:**
The champion (score 3903) uses:
- State 0: Exploration with rwm=20 (slow turning = mostly straight movement)
- Two transitions from State 0: (1) perceive green (l=2) with p=0.95 → go to Stop, (2) random with p=0.0005 → also go to Stop
- State 1: Stop with tiny random exit probability (0.001)

Key insight: rwm=20 (low turning rate) means mostly straight-line movement, which is good for moving before the switch. The green perception (l=2) triggers the stop.

**What to tune:**
1. The rwm value in state 0 - try values near 20 (10, 15, 25, 30)
2. The green detection probability (0.95 seems good, try 0.99, 1.0 isn't allowed but try 0.98)
3. The fallback random transition probability (5e-04 seems very good)
4. The stop state exit probability (0.001 - try making it 0.0 effectively, or slightly different)
5. Try adding neighbor-count transition (if many neighbors seen, they might have found green wall)

**Exploitation (7 machines):**
- Champion verbatim
- Vary rwm: 10, 15, 25
- Vary green detection p: 0.98, 0.99
- Vary random fallback: 0.0001, 0.001

**Exploration (6 machines):**
- 3-state: explore → stop, with a neighbor-based transition too
- Try adding a second color check (maybe robots with LED color signal the switch)
- Try GoToColor green as intermediate state
- Try Repulsion in state 0 (also moves)

Note: The destination indexing - with 2 states, leaving state 0, D=0 means state 1 (the only other state). Leaving state 1, D=0 means state 0. This is what the champion uses correctly.

For 3-state machines: leaving state 0, others are [1,2] so D=0→state1, D=1→state2. Leaving state 1, others are [0,2] so D=0→state0, D=1→state2. Leaving state 2, others are [0,1] so D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

# ROUND 4/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings:**
- rwm=1 (minimal turning = nearly straight-line movement) is dramatically better than rwm=10, 15, 20
- The core structure works: Explore (rwm=1) → Stop on green (l=2, p=0.95) + tiny random fallback (5e-04)
- Stop state with tiny exit probability (0.001) works well
- Lower exit probability from stop (1e-04) also works well (rank 3)

**Why rwm=1 is best:** With rwm=1, the robot goes almost perfectly straight, covering maximum distance and thus being maximally "moving" before the switch. This minimizes the pre-switch penalty.

**What to explore:**
1. Can we do better than rwm=1? It's the minimum, so no lower value exists.
2. Tune the green detection probability: try 0.98, 0.99 (currently 0.95)
3. Tune the random fallback: currently 5e-04, try 1e-04, 1e-03, 0 (but 0 might strand robots if green isn't visible)
4. Tune the stop exit probability: 0.001 vs 1e-04 - both worked, try even lower
5. Try GoToColor green (B=8) as the moving state - robots actively seek the green wall, stopping faster after switch
6. Try adding neighbor-based transitions
7. Try 3-state machines with rwm=1

**Important:** The random fallback (5e-04) from state 0 to stop might actually hurt - it causes some robots to stop before seeing green. But it might help if green isn't visible. Keep it but also try without.

**Exploitation (8 machines):**
- Champion verbatim
- rwm=1 + p_green=0.98, 0.99
- rwm=1 + fallback=1e-04, 1e-03
- rwm=1 + stop_exit=1e-04
- rwm=1 + no random fallback (single transition on green only)
- rwm=1 + p_green=0.95 + stop_exit=0.0001

**Exploration (5 machines):**
- GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2) → Stop: robots approach green wall then stop
- 3-state with rwm=1
- Attraction state → Stop on green
- rwm=1 with neighbor count transition added

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings so far:**
- Champion: rwm=1, explore→stop on green (p=0.95), random fallback (5e-04), stop exit probability=1e-04
- The stop exit probability of 1e-04 is better than 0.001 (champion improved from 2484 to 2395)
- Having a random fallback from explore state (5e-04) is important - pure green-only transition (worst #2: 2797) is worse
- Adding a transition from stop back on seeing green (worst #3: 4527) is bad - it makes robots move again after stopping
- The topology is quite converged: 2-state Explore→Stop

**What to tune next:**
1. Stop exit probability: 1e-04 is best so far, try 5e-05, 1e-05, 0 (but 0 isn't valid, min is probably any positive float)
2. Green detection probability: 0.95 is in champion, 0.98 also performed well (#4), try 0.97, 0.96
3. Random fallback: 5e-04 is best, try 2e-04, 3e-04, 7e-04
4. The 3-state machine (#3: 2588) with a backup explore state was decent

**Critical insight:** The score of ~2395 means roughly 2395 robot-steps of penalty. The switch happens at 400-600, so average 500 steps. Before switch: 10 robots × 500 steps = 5000 potential points if all stationary. After switch: 10 robots × 700 steps = 7000 potential points if all moving. The champion gets ~2395, so it's doing well but not perfect. 

The remaining gap: robots might not see green immediately (it's one wall, not all walls), so some delay in stopping. Also the random fallback causes some premature stops.

**Ideas:**
- Try very tiny stop exit: 5e-05 or 1e-05 (once stopped, almost never restart)
- Try p_green=0.99 for faster stopping
- Try adding a neighbor-count trigger to stop (if many robots stopped = green was found by neighbors)
- Try GoToColor green as intermediate state to get closer to green faster

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings:**
- Champion: rwm=1, green detection p=0.95, random fallback=7e-04, stop exit=1e-04 → score 2132.67
- p=0 for stop exit also works well (rank 3: 2211) - once stopped, stay stopped
- p_fallback=0.001 is also good (rank 2: 2139)
- Adding neighbor-count transition from explore state (worst #1: 4916) is bad
- Adding transitions from stop state (worst #3: 6210) is terrible

**Parameter tuning to try:**
1. Stop exit probability: 1e-04 is champion, 0 also works → try 5e-05, 2e-05
2. Random fallback: 7e-04 champion, 0.001 also good → try 8e-04, 9e-04, 6e-04
3. Green detection: 0.95 is champion, but 0.97 and 0.99 also good → the range 0.95-0.99 seems optimal
4. Combination: try p_green=0.97 + fallback=7e-04 + stop_exit=1e-04

**Important observations:**
- Score ~2132 means significant improvement still possible. Theoretical minimum: if robots move perfectly before switch (~500 steps) and stop instantly after, score ≈ 0. But green wall is only one wall, so not all robots see it immediately.
- The random fallback (7e-04) makes some robots stop randomly before seeing green - this is actually helping because it reduces movement after switch for robots that never see the green wall

**Exploration ideas:**
- Try very small stop exit (essentially permanent stop once triggered)
- Try p_green higher (0.98) with various fallbacks
- Consider if there's any signaling mechanism - robots could signal with LEDs when they see green, and others respond to seeing that LED color

**LED signaling idea:** If a robot sees green wall, it transitions to stop AND changes LED color. Other robots in explore state could also detect that LED color and stop. This could propagate the stop signal!

Let me try: State 0 (explore, cle=4=yellow) → Stop on green (cle=5=magenta). State 0 also has C=7 check for magenta (l=5) to stop when neighbors have stopped. But wait - the stop state needs cle, and if I use cle=5 (magenta) on stop state, exploring robots with C=7 l=5 could detect stopped robots and also stop.

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings:**
- Champion remains at 2132.67 - no improvement this round despite many variations
- The champion parameters seem near-optimal: rwm=1, p_green=0.95, fallback=7e-04, stop_exit=1e-04
- The 3-state LED signaling machine (rank 1: 2306) is interesting - it uses magenta LED (cle=5) on stop state, and explore state checks for magenta (l=5) to propagate stopping. But it's worse than champion.
- stop_exit=0 (rank 4: 2329) is slightly worse than 1e-04 - so some tiny exit is helpful
- fallback values: 7e-04 (champion) > 9e-04 > 0.001 in this round, but previous rounds showed 0.001 was comparable
- 8e-04 (worst #1: 2420) is surprisingly bad for this round - stochastic noise

**Analysis of what's left to try:**
The champion is very close to optimal for this structure. The remaining gap (~2132) could be:
- Robots that don't see green until several steps after the switch (1 wall visible only from certain angles)
- The random fallback causing ~7e-04 × 10 robots × 500 steps ≈ 3.5 premature stops before switch

**New ideas to break out of local optimum:**
1. Try having the stop state use a non-zero LED and have exploring robots also detect that LED - but previous attempt (worst #3) was terrible because it caused oscillation
2. The 3-state LED signaling worked reasonably (2306) - try refining it: lower the magenta detection probability to reduce false stops
3. Try p_green=0.95 + fallback=7e-04 with even lower stop_exit (1e-05)
4. Try fallback values between 5e-04 and 9e-04 more carefully: 6e-04 wasn't tested this round
5. What if we use GoToColor green (B=8) briefly to orient toward green wall faster?
6. Try a 3-state: explore → GoToColor(green) → stop, where GoToColor transition to stop on seeing green

**Exploitation (8 machines):**
- Champion verbatim
- Vary fallback: 6e-04, 5e-04, 4e-04
- Vary stop_exit: 1e-05, 5e-05, 2e-04
- Vary p_green: 0.96

**Exploration (4 machines):**
- 3-state LED signaling with lower magenta probability (0.5 instead of 0.9)
- 3-state: explore → GoToColor(green) → stop
- 2-state with GoToColor green as explore state (B=8, clr=2)
- 3-state: fast explore (rwm=1) → stop on green, with a secondary explore state

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Situation assessment:**
- Champion stuck at 2132.67 for 3 rounds now - we're in a local optimum
- The core structure is firmly established: rwm=1 explore → stop on green (l=2, p=0.95) + random fallback (7e-04), stop with tiny exit (1e-04)
- Stochasticity is high - the same machine scores 2132 in one run, 2379 in another
- The LED signaling 3-state (rank 2: 2365) keeps appearing as viable

**Why the score can't easily go lower:**
- The green wall is only 1 of N walls - robots face random directions initially
- A robot exploring straight (rwm=1) will eventually face the green wall, but might take many steps
- The random fallback (7e-04) causes ~7e-04 × 10 × 500 = 3.5 premature stops before switch
- After switch: robots not yet stopped need to see green wall

**What might help:**
1. **Faster green detection:** robots need to see green sooner. With rwm=1 they go straight but might face away from green wall. What if we also use GoToColor(green) so they actively seek it?
2. **Neighbor signaling:** once one robot stops (having seen green), others should stop too. The challenge is distinguishing "stopped because saw green" from "stopped randomly."
3. **Try different color for signal:** use color 2 (green) as LED for stopped robots, then other robots detect LED color 2 (but this might confuse with wall green... actually robot LEDs and wall colors are different perceptions)

Actually, looking at C=7 - it detects colored objects with the camera. Robot LEDs would be visible to other robots. So if stopped robots have green LED (cle=2... wait, cle options are {0,4,5,6}). The LED colors are limited: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So we CAN'T use green (2) as LED color! The LED signaling with magenta (5) is the only colored option that robots can detect in neighbors.

**The magenta signal idea:** Stop state uses magenta LED (cle=5). Exploring robots detect magenta (l=5) from neighbors and also stop. This worked somewhat (2306, 2365). Let me refine it.

**Key issue with magenta signaling:** The random fallback causes some robots to enter stop (magenta) before the switch, causing others to cascade-stop prematurely. Fix: remove random fallback when using magenta signaling, rely purely on green wall detection + magenta neighbor detection.

**New approach:** 
- State 0 (explore, rwm=1, cle=4=yellow): transition to stop when seeing green (l=2) OR seeing magenta neighbors (l=5)
- State 1 (stop, cle=5=magenta): tiny exit probability

But we must not use random fallback (5e-04) in this version since it would trigger false magenta cascade.

**Other exploration:** 
- Try different fallback values between 6e-04 and 8e-04 more carefully
- Try stop_exit=0 (permanent stop)
- Try 3-state with GoToColor green as intermediate

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**MASSIVE breakthrough!** Score dropped from ~2132 to 292! The LED signaling mechanism works brilliantly:

**Champion mechanism:**
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1, yellow LED=4) with two transitions:
  1. See green wall (l=2, p=0.95) → go to Stop
  2. See magenta (l=5, p=0.8) → go to Stop (neighbor signaling!)
- State 1: Stop (magenta LED=5) with tiny exit (1e-04)

**Why it works so well:** One robot sees green → stops → turns magenta → neighbors see magenta → they stop too → cascade! This propagates the stop signal to ALL robots very quickly, even those not facing the green wall.

**Key parameters to tune:**
1. Magenta detection probability: 0.8 (champion) vs 0.6 (rank 2: 298) - both excellent
2. Green detection probability: 0.95 - keep
3. Stop exit: 1e-04 - keep
4. No random fallback in champion - good, avoids premature cascade

**What to explore:**
- Try p_magenta: 0.7, 0.75, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95 (fine-tune around 0.6-0.8)
- Try p_green: 0.97, 0.99 (detect green faster)
- Try stop_exit: 5e-05, 0 (more stable stop)
- Try adding random fallback back (tiny) to help robots that never see green or magenta
- Try 3-state with magenta signaling (rank 3: 2149 was much worse, so keep 2-state)
- Try using cyan (l=6) instead of magenta for signal to see if color matters (it shouldn't but worth checking)
- Try p_magenta very high (0.99) for faster cascade

**Important:** The 3-state version (rank 3: 2149) was much worse - stick with 2-state.
The version with random fallback AND magenta (rank 4: 2298) was worse - the random fallback causes premature cascade.

**Exploitation (9 machines):**
- Champion verbatim
- Vary p_magenta: 0.7, 0.75, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95
- Vary p_green: 0.97, 0.99
- Vary stop_exit: 5e-05

**Exploration (3 machines):**
- Try cyan signal (cle=6, l=6) instead of magenta
- Try adding tiny random fallback (1e-04) to help isolated robots
- Try p_magenta=0.99 for near-instant cascade

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**

The design has converged beautifully. All top performers use the same 2-state structure:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1, yellow LED=4), transitions on green wall (l=2, p=0.95) OR magenta neighbors (l=5, p=0.8-0.99)
- State 1: Stop (magenta LED=5), tiny exit probability (1e-04 or 0)

**Key observations:**
- Higher magenta detection probability is consistently better: 0.99 > 0.95 > 0.9 > 0.85 > 0.8 > 0.75 > 0.7
- This makes sense: faster cascade = all robots stop sooner after switch
- Adding random fallback (worst #3: 1081) is terrible - premature cascade before switch
- stop_exit=0 (rank 4: 307) is comparable to 1e-04
- The score range is very tight (292-311) for all reasonable parameter settings

**For this final round:**
- Focus on p_magenta in range 0.95-0.99 (sweet spot)
- Try p_magenta=1.0 isn't valid (must be [0,1], so try 0.999)
- Try p_green higher: 0.97, 0.99 combined with high p_magenta
- Try stop_exit=0 vs 1e-04 vs 5e-05
- Try adding a second green detection: what if stop state also has transition back if green disappears? (No - that was shown to be bad)
- Try cyan signal instead of magenta (just in case)
- The 3-state with random fallback was terrible - avoid

**Exploitation (9 machines):**
- Champion verbatim
- p_magenta: 0.97, 0.98, 0.999
- p_green: 0.97+p_magenta=0.99, 0.99+p_magenta=0.99
- stop_exit: 0, 5e-05 with best p_magenta
- p_magenta=0.99 + stop_exit=0

**Exploration (2 machines):**
- Try cyan (cle=6, l=6) signal
- Try both green and magenta p very high with stop_exit=0

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 10 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.75 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
